\displaystyle\Upsilon_{a}\Upsilon_{b}=

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{b}\Upsilon_{a},

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{a}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{a}^{\top},

\displaystyle\eta_{a}\Upsilon_{b}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Upsilon_{b}\eta_{a}&a\neq b,\;a,b\in\{1,\dots,D\},\\
\Upsilon_{b}^{-1}\eta_{a}&a=b,\end{cases},

and the very important spectral property

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{a}\chi(\boldsymbol{z})=

\displaystyle z_{a}\chi(\boldsymbol{z}).

Proof.

See Appendix
C.4
.
∎

For examples with
D=1,2
see Appendix
B
.

Definition 2.21
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
we introduce the matrix

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\coloneq\prod_{a=1}^{D}\big(\Upsilon_{a}\big)^{\alpha_{a}}.

Proposition 2.21
.

For the spectral matrices the following properties hold

(1)

Given a multi-index
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
we have
\Upsilon_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=(\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})^{-1}=\big(\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\big)^{\top}=\eta\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\eta
; i.e.,
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is an orthogonal persymmetric block matrix.

(2)

\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is a
2|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+1
-diagonal block matrix, with at most
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
block superdiagonals and
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
block subdiagonal. If
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
is odd, the main diagonal and the even superdiagonals and subdiagonals vanish, if
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
is even then the odd block superdiagonals and subdiagonals are zero.

(3)

For any couple of multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
we have
\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}}=\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}}\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}}
.

(4)

The following eigen-value property holds
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\chi(\boldsymbol{z})=\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\chi(\boldsymbol{z})
.

Proof.

(1)

From (
2.12
) and the properties of the exchange matrix
\eta
we get

\displaystyle\eta\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\eta=\prod_{a=1}^{D}\big(\eta\Upsilon_{a}\eta\big)^{\alpha_{a}}

and recalling that
\{\Upsilon_{a}\}_{a=1}^{D}
is an Abelian set of real persymmetric orthogonal matrices; i.e.,
\eta\Upsilon_{a}\eta=\Upsilon_{a}^{\top}=\Upsilon_{a}^{-1}
we deduce that we get

\displaystyle\eta\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\eta=

\displaystyle\prod_{a=1}^{D}\big(\Upsilon_{a}^{\top}\big)^{\alpha_{a}}=\prod_{a=1}^{D}\big(\Upsilon_{a}^{-1}\big)^{-\alpha_{a}}=\prod_{a=1}^{D}\big(\Upsilon_{a}\big)^{-\alpha_{a}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\top}=\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{-1}=\Upsilon_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}.

(2)

As
\Upsilon_{a}
are tri-diagonal block matrices then the product of
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
of them will be a
2|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+1
-diagonal block matrix, and recalling that the main diagonal vanishes in
\Upsilon_{a}
we deduce the rest of the statement.

(3)

It is a consequence of (
2.12
) and the commutativity of the
\Upsilon_{a}
among them.

(4)

Follows also from the eigen-value property described in Proposition
2.20
.

∎

We are now ready to show a very important symmetry

Proposition 2.22

(The string equations)
.

For any multi-index
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
the moment matrix satisfies

(2.13)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}G=G\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}.

Proof.

It is a consequence of (
3.3
) and the following sequence of equalities

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}G=

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\top}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big(\chi\big(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)\big)^{\top}\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}.

∎

2.8.
Three-term relations

The symmetries of the moment matrix suggest

Definition 2.22
.

We consider the Jacobi matrices

\displaystyle J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\coloneq S\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\hat{S}\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\hat{S}^{-1}

and the reversal Jacobi matrices

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\bar{\hat{S}}\eta\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{-1}.

Proposition 2.23
.

The following properties hold true

(1)

J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=H\hat{J}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}H^{-1}
.

(2)

J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is a
(2|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+1)
-diagonal block semi-infinite matrix

(3)

We have
J_{0}=\mathbb{I}
.

(4)

For any couple of multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
we have
J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}}=J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}}J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}}
. In particular,
(J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})^{-1}=J_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}

\forall{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
.

(5)

We have

\displaystyle C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle=H^{\top}C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{\top}H^{-1},

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=\eta,

\displaystyle C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=S\eta\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\big(\bar{\hat{S}}\big)^{-1},

and consequently
C_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is a
(2|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+1)
-diagonal block semi-infinite matrix.

(6)

The following properties are satisfied

\displaystyle\eta J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle=C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}},

\displaystyle\eta\bar{\hat{J}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{-1},

\displaystyle\eta J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\eta

\displaystyle=\bar{\hat{J}}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.5
.
∎

Proposition 2.24
.

The Jacobi matrices satisfy the following eigen-value relations

\displaystyle J_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Phi(z),

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}),

and the following relations are fulfilled as well

\displaystyle C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\bar{\hat{\Phi}}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1}),

\displaystyle C_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{-1}\bar{\hat{\Phi}}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1}).

The basic Jacobi matrices
J_{a}=S\Upsilon_{a}S^{-1}
are tri-diagonal block semi-infinite matrices

\displaystyle J_{a}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}(J_{a})_{[0],[0]}&(J_{a})_{[0],[1]}&0&0&0&\dots\\
(J_{a})_{[1],[0]}&(J_{a})_{[1],[1]}&(J_{a})_{[1],[2]}&0&0&\dots\\
0&(J_{a})_{[2],[1]}&(J_{a})_{[2],[2]}&(J_{a})_{[2],[3]}&0&\dots\\
0&0&(J_{a})_{[3],[2]}&(J_{a})_{[3],[3]}&(J_{a})_{[3],[4]}&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ddots&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ddots&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\ddots&\end{matrix}\right)

with blocks given in terms of quasi-tau matrices and first subdiagonal coefficients by

(2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (J_{a})_{[0][0]}&=-(\Upsilon_{a})_{[0],[1]}\beta_{[1]}=-H_{[0]}\hat{\beta}_{[1]}^{\dagger}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[1],[0]}H_{[0]}^{-1},\\
(J_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}&=H_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1},\\
(J_{a})_{[k][k]}&=\beta_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k-1],[k]}-(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}\beta_{[k+1]}\\
&=H_{[k]}\big[(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}\hat{\beta}^{\dagger}_{[k]}-\hat{\beta}^{\dagger}_{[k+1]}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k+1],[k]}\big]H_{[k]}^{-1}\\
(J_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}&=(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

Similarly,
\hat{J}_{a}
has the same form and the blocks are obtained from the previous formulæ with the replacements

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow H^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\hat{\beta}.

The matrices
C_{a}
are tri-diagonal with blocks given by

\displaystyle(C_{a})_{[0][0]}

\displaystyle=-(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[0][1]}\beta_{[1]}=-H_{[0]}^{\top}\beta_{[1]}^{\top}(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[1][0]}H_{[0]}^{-1},

\displaystyle(C_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}

\displaystyle=H_{[k]}^{\top}(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1},

\displaystyle(C_{a})_{[k][k]}

\displaystyle=\bar{\hat{\beta}}_{[k]}(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k-1],[k]}-(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}\beta_{[k+1]}

\displaystyle=H_{[k]}^{\top}\big[(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}\bar{\hat{\beta}}^{\top}_{[k]}-\beta^{\top}_{[k+1]}(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k+1],[k]}\big]H_{[k]}^{-1},

\displaystyle(C_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}

\displaystyle=(\eta\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k+1]},

and
C_{a}^{-1}
is also tri-diagonal with blocks as above with the replacement given by the following prescription

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow H^{\top},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\bar{\hat{\beta}}.

Definition 2.23
.

With the notation

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{-a}

\displaystyle\coloneq\Upsilon_{a}^{-1},

\displaystyle a\in\{1,\dots,D\},

the vector

\boldsymbol{n}=(n_{-1},\dots,n_{-D},n_{D},\dots,n_{1})^{\top}\in\mathbb{C}^{2D}

and
\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}\coloneq\mathcal{E}_{2D}\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}

we consider:

(1)

associated longitude one homogeneous Laurent polynomials

\displaystyle L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{a=1}^{D}\frac{n_{-a}}{z_{a}}+\sum_{a=1}^{D}n_{a}z_{a},

\displaystyle L_{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{a=1}^{D}\bar{n}_{-a}z_{a}+\sum_{a=1}^{D}\frac{\bar{n}_{a}}{z_{a}}.

(2)

associated slashed semi-infinite matrices

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}

\displaystyle\equiv L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\coloneq\sum_{a=1}^{D}(n_{-a}\Upsilon_{a}^{-1}+n_{a}\Upsilon_{a}),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}

\displaystyle\equiv L_{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\coloneq\sum_{a=1}^{D}\bar{n}_{-a}\Upsilon_{a}+\sum_{a=1}^{D}\bar{n}^{+}_{a}\Upsilon_{a}^{-1}.

Observe that

Proposition 2.25
.

The following formulæ holds

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}\chi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=n(\boldsymbol{z})\chi(\boldsymbol{z}).

Proposition 2.26

(Three-term relations)
.

The MVOLPUT satisfy the following recursion type relations

\displaystyle L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z})\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=H_{[k]}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\big(\beta_{[k]}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k-1],[k]}-\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k-1]}\beta_{[k+1]}\big)\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k+1]}\phi_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z})\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=(H_{[k]})^{\dagger}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k-1]}(H_{[k-1]}^{-1})^{\dagger}\hat{\phi}_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\big(\hat{\beta}_{[k]}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k-1],[k]}-\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k-1]}\hat{\beta}_{[k+1]}\big)\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k+1]}\hat{\phi}_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}),

and its conjugate partners

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z})\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})=H_{[k]}^{\top}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\big(\bar{\hat{\beta}}_{[k]}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k-1],[k]}-(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k+1]}\beta_{[k+1]}\big)\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})\\
+(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k+1]}\phi_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}),\ \end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z})\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})=H_{[k]}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k-1]}\big(H_{[k-1]}^{\top}\big)^{-1}\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})+\big(\beta_{[k]}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k-1],[k]}-(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k+1]}\bar{\hat{\beta}}_{[k+1]}\big)\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})\\
+(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k+1]}\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}).\end{multlined}

2.9.
Christoffel–Darboux formulæ

Definition 2.24
.

The Christoffel–Darboux kernel is defined by

\displaystyle K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\coloneq\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\left(\hat{\phi}_{[l]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\right)^{\dagger}H_{[l]}^{-1}\phi_{[l]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})

Proposition 2.27
.

Both sets of MVOLPUT
\big\{\phi_{q}\big\}_{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leq k}
and
\big\{\hat{\phi}_{q}\big\}_{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leq k}
are linear basis for the linear space
\mathbb{C}_{k}[\boldsymbol{z}^{\pm 1}]
of Laurent polynomials of longitude less or equal to
k
.

Thus, a Laurent polynomial
L
can be always expanded as

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\\
0\leqslant|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\ll\infty\end{subarray}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{\phi}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\\
0\leqslant|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\ll\infty\end{subarray}}\lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\boldsymbol{z}),

with coefficients
\hat{\lambda}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}},\lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\in\mathbb{C}
.

The corresponding projections are

Definition 2.25
.

We introduce the two projectors onto
\mathbb{C}^{[k]}[\boldsymbol{z}^{\pm 1}]
as the following
k
-th truncated sums

\displaystyle\Pi^{(k)}(L)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\\
0\leqslant|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}^{(k)}(L)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\\
0\leqslant|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{\phi}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}(\boldsymbol{z}),

Due to the biorthogonality (
2.7
) we have

Proposition 2.28
.

(1)

The coefficients in the MVOLPUT expansions of a Laurent polynomial
L
are given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}|=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})\overline{\hat{\phi}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})}(H^{-1})_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}|=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}(H^{-1})_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})\overline{\phi_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})}\operatorname{d}\bar{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(2)

The projections given in Definition
2.25
can be expressed in terms of the Christoffel–Darboux kernel as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(k)}(L)(\boldsymbol{z})=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})K^{(k+1)}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{z})\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}^{(k)}(L)(\boldsymbol{z})=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\overline{K^{(k+1)}(\boldsymbol{z},\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})}L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})\operatorname{d}\bar{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Therefore, these kernels are the integral kernels of the projection operators in the space of multivariate Laurent polynomials of longitude equal or less than
k
. This interpretation leads at once to

Proposition 2.29

(Reproducing property)
.

K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})K^{(k)}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})
.

From the Gauss–Borel factorization we deduce

Proposition 2.30

(ABC Theorem)
.

An Aitken–Berg–Coller type formula

\displaystyle K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\big(\chi^{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})\big)^{\dagger}(G^{[k]})^{-1}\chi^{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),

holds.

Proposition 2.31
.

The Christoffel–Darboux kernel satisfies

\displaystyle K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=K^{(k)}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{2}^{-1},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1}).

Proof.

It is a consequence of (
2.11
) and the persymmetry of
H
:

\displaystyle K^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\big(\phi_{[l]}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})\big)^{\top}\mathcal{E}_{|[l]|}H^{-1}_{[l]}\phi_{[l]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{=0}^{k-1}(\hat{\phi}_{[l]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}H^{-1}_{[l]}\mathcal{E}_{|[l]|}\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[l]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}^{-1})

\displaystyle=K^{(k)}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{2}^{-1},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1}).

∎

Proposition 2.32
.

The Christoffel–Darboux type formulae hold

\displaystyle K^{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})-(\hat{\phi}_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}_{[k-1],[k]}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})}{L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})-L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\phi_{[k]}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1}))^{\top}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})-(\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}^{-1}))^{\top}(H_{[k-1]}^{\top})^{-1}(\eta\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}})_{[k-1],[k]}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})}{L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})-L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}(\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}\eta)_{[k],[k-1]}(H_{[k-1]}^{\top})^{-1}\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}^{-1})-(\hat{\phi}_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}\eta)_{[k-1],[k]}\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}^{-1})}{L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})-L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})}.

Proof.

In order to get the first of the three expressions one has to consider the two possible ways of computing
the expression
(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}\left[(H^{-1}(L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k+1]}\right]\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})
letting the operator between brackets
act on the polynomial located on its right or left hand side; i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}\left[(H^{-1}(L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k+1]}\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\right]=L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}(H^{[k+1]})^{-1}\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\\
-(\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}H_{[k]}^{-1}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k][k+1]}\phi_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\left[(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}(H^{-1}L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k+1]}\right]\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=L_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}(H^{[k+1]})^{-1}\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})\\
-(\hat{\phi}_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k+1][k]}H_{[k]}^{-1}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}).\end{multlined}

Since both results must coincide, subtracting both we get the desired expression. The remaining two equalities can
be proven in two different ways. The first and simpler one is to use the expression we have just proven and remember the relations
between
\phi
and
\hat{\phi}
established by
\eta
. A different approach is to compare the two possible ways of computing

\displaystyle(\phi^{[k+1]}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}^{-1})^{\top}\{([H^{\top}]^{-1}C_{a})^{[k+1]}\}\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2})

and

\displaystyle(\hat{\phi}^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}))^{\dagger}\left[([C_{a}^{-1}]^{\top}[H^{-1}]^{\top})^{[k+1]}\right]\phi^{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}).

∎

3.
Darboux transformations

We now discuss perturbations of the measure given by the multiplication by a Laurent polynomial. This is the extension, in the multivariate Laurent orthogonal polynomial setting of the Christoffel transformation, or in a geometric-differenttial setting of a Lèvy transformation, or in the theory of integrable systems of a direct Darboux transformation.

3.1.
Laurent polynomial perturbations of the measure

Definition 3.1
.

Given a multivariate Laurent polynomial
the corresponding Darboux transformation of the measure is the following perturbed measure

(3.1)

\displaystyle T\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=L(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Proposition 3.1
.

The perturbed measure
L\operatorname{d}\mu
is real if and only if

\bar{L}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})=L(\boldsymbol{z})
;
i.e., when the Laurent polynomial can be written as

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{z})=L_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sum_{i=1}^{|[k]|/2}(L_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}z^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}+\bar{L}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}z^{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}).

When we have

\displaystyle L_{0}>2\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sum_{i=1}^{|[k]|/2}|L_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}|

definite positiveness of
T\operatorname{d}\mu
is ensured.

Proof.

The perturbed the measure can be written

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\Big(L_{0}+2\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sum_{i=1}^{|[k]|/2}|L_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}|\cos\big({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}+\arg L_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{(k)}}\big)\Big)\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

from where the sufficient condition for positiveness follows.
∎

Another manner of ensuring positivity is as follows. For
D=1
the Fejér-Riesz factorization
[
39
,
77
]
allows us for expressing a Laurent polynomial which is non negative in the circle
\mathbb{T}
in the following manner
L(z)=\bar{Q}(z^{-1})Q(z)
, for a polynomial
Q
; notice that this expression when evaluated on the circle
\mathbb{T}
takes the form

L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta})=|Q(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta})|^{2}
.
In the multivariate scenario the situation is quite different. As proven in
[
36
]
and discussed further in
[
48
]
Fejér-Riesz factorization can be extended; indeed, we can write any multivariate Laurent polynomial strictly positive in
\mathbb{T}^{D}

as the Dirtschel’s finite sum of squared magnitudes of multivariate polynomials

(3.2)

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{z})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bar{Q}_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})Q_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle L(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}|Q_{i}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})|^{2}.

Proposition 3.2
.

The moment matrices
TG
and
G
satisfy

\displaystyle TG=L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})G=G\;L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}).

Proof.

We prove the first equality

(3.3)

\displaystyle TG=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))(T\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\chi(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}

from Definition
2.6

(3.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}\chi(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))(L(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\chi(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}

from (
3.1
)

(3.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}}(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\chi)(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta})\chi(\boldsymbol{z}(-\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}

from (
2.20
)

(3.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})G.

The second equation follows similarly.
∎

We proceed to introduce a semi-infinite matrix that models the Darboux transformation

Definition 3.2
.

The resolvents are

\displaystyle\omega\coloneq

\displaystyle(TS)L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})S^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}^{\dagger}\coloneq\big(\hat{S}^{-1}\big)^{\dagger}L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})(T\hat{S})^{\dagger}.

We also introduce

Definition 3.3
.

The adjoint resolvents are

\displaystyle\hat{M}

\displaystyle\coloneq\hat{S}T\hat{S}^{-1},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\coloneq STS^{-1}.

Proposition 3.3
.

The resolvents and the adjoint resolvents satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{M}^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle(TH^{-1})\omega H,

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle H\hat{\omega}^{\dagger}(TH)^{-1}.

Proof.

Proposition
3.3
and the corresponding Gauss–Borel factorizations lead to

\displaystyle(TS)^{-1}(TH)\big((T\hat{S})^{-1}\big)^{\dagger}=L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})S^{-1}H\big(\hat{S}^{-1})^{\dagger}=S^{-1}H\big(\hat{S}^{-1})^{\dagger}L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}),

and an appropriate cleaning of these equations to

\displaystyle(TH)\big(\hat{S}(T\hat{S})^{-1}\big)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(TS)L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})S^{-1}H,

\displaystyle S(TS)^{-1}(TH)

\displaystyle=H\big(\hat{S}^{-1})^{\dagger}L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})(T\hat{S})^{\dagger},

from where the result follows.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

The resolvents
\omega
and
\hat{\omega}
are block upper triangular matrices having all their block superdiagonals above the
m
-th block superdiagonal equal to zero. The adjoint resolvents
M
and
\hat{M}
are block lower unitriangular matrices having all their block subdiagonals below the
m
-th block subdiagonal equal to zero.

Proof.

The reader should notice that, if the longitude of the perturbing Laurent polynomial is
\ell(L)=m
, the semi-infinite matrix
L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})
is a
(2m+1)
-banded matrix with only the first
m
block superdiagonals, the main diagonal and the first
m
subdiagonals different from zero; hence,
Definition
3.2
implies that
\omega
has all its superdiagonals above the
m
-th superdiagonal equal to zero and that
\hat{\omega}^{\dagger}
has all its subdiagonals below the
m
-th subdiagonal equal to zero.
Observe also that Definition
3.3
implies that the adjoint resolvents
M

and
\hat{M}
are block lower unitriangular matrices.
Now, Proposition
3.3
linking resolvents and adjoint resolvents gives the satated result.
∎

Regarding the deformed MVOLPUT and the original ones we have

Proposition 3.5
.

The following relations among Darboux perturbed and original MVOLPUT hold true

(3.7)

\displaystyle\omega\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=L(\boldsymbol{z})T\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\bar{L}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})T\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}),

(3.8)

\displaystyle MT\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{M}T\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}),

Proof.

To prove equations (
3.7
) just observe that

\displaystyle\omega\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=(TS)L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})S^{-1}S\chi=(TS)L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\chi(\boldsymbol{z})=L(\boldsymbol{z})T\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=(T\hat{S})(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))^{\dagger}\hat{S}^{-1}S\chi=(T\hat{S})(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))^{\dagger}\chi(\boldsymbol{z})=\bar{L}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})T\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}).

The relations (
3.8
) are a consequence of Proposition
3.3
.
∎

3.2.
Interpolation, sample matrices and poised sets

Definition 3.4
.

The algebraic hypersurface of zeroes of the Laurent polynomial
L
in the algebraic torus is denoted by

\displaystyle Z(L)\coloneq\big\{\boldsymbol{p}\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D}:L(\boldsymbol{p})=0\big\}.

Definition 3.5
.

A set of nodes in the algebraic torus

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{k,m}\coloneq\{\boldsymbol{p}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{r_{k,m}}\subset(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D}

is a set with
r_{k,m}\coloneq N_{k+m-1}-N_{k-1}=|[k]|+\dots+|[k+m-1]|
nodes. Given these nodes we consider
the corresponding sample matrices

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{k}^{m}\coloneq

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})&\dots&\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{r_{k,m}})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\phi_{[k+m-1]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})&\dots&\phi_{[k+m-1]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{r_{k,m}})\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{r_{k,m}\times r_{k,m}},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{[k,m]}\coloneq

\displaystyle\big(\phi_{[k+m]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1}),\dots,\phi_{[k+m]}(\boldsymbol{p}_{r_{k,m}})\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{|[k+m]|\times r_{k,m}}.

Lemma 3.1
.

When the set of nodes
\mathcal{N}_{k,m}\subset Z(L)
belongs to the algebraic hypersurface of the Laurent polynomial
L
of longitude
m=\ell(L)
, the resolvent coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle\omega_{[k],[k+m]}\Sigma_{[k,m]}+(\omega_{[k],[k]},\dots,\omega_{[k],[k+m-1]})\Sigma_{k}^{m}=0.

Now we introduce an important type of node sets within
the algebraic hypersurface of the Laurent polynomial
L
of longitude
m=\ell(L)

Definition 3.6
.

We say that the set
\mathcal{N}_{k,m}\subset Z(L)
is a poised set if the sample matrix given in (
3.9
) is non singular

\displaystyle\det\Sigma_{k}^{m}\neq 0.

Theorem 3.1

(A Christoffel formula)
.

For a poised set of nodes
\mathcal{N}_{k,m}\subset Z(L)
the Darboux transformation of the MVOLPUT can be expressed in terms of the original ones as the following last quasi-determinantal expression

\displaystyle T\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})_{[k],[k+m]}}{L(\boldsymbol{z})}\Theta_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\Sigma^{m}_{k}&\begin{matrix}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{[k+m-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\Sigma_{[k,m]}&\phi_{[k+m]}(\boldsymbol{z})\end{array}\right).

Proof.

Observe that Lemma
3.1
together with
\omega_{[k],[k+m]}=\big(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\big)_{[k],[k+m]}
implies

\displaystyle(\omega_{[k],[k]},\dots,\omega_{[k],[k+m-1]})=-\big(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\big)_{[k],[k+m]}\Sigma_{[k,m]}\big(\Sigma_{k}^{m}\big)^{-1}.

and from
L(\boldsymbol{z})T\phi(\boldsymbol{z})=\omega\phi(\boldsymbol{z})
the result follows.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

The resolvent coefficients can be expressed as last quasi-determinants

\displaystyle\omega_{[k],[k+l]}=(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m]}\Theta_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{matrix}\Sigma^{m}_{k}\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}0_{[k],[k+l]}\\
\vdots\\
0_{[k+l-1],[k+l]}\\
\mathbb{I}_{[k+l]}\\
0_{[k+l+1],[k+l]}\\
\vdots\end{matrix},\\
\hline\cr\Sigma_{[k,m]}&0_{[k+m],[k+l]}\end{array}\right).

for
l=0,\dots,m-1
.

Proposition 3.7
.

We have the following Darboux transformation formulæ for the quasi-tau matrices

\displaystyle TH_{[k]}=

\displaystyle(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m]}\Theta_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{matrix}\Sigma^{m}_{k}\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}H_{[k]}\\
0_{[k+1],[k]}\\
\vdots\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\Sigma_{[k,m]}&0_{[k+m],[k]}\end{array}\right).

and for the transformed first subdiagonal coefficients

\displaystyle(T\beta)_{[k]}(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k-1],[k+m-1]}=(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m-1]}+L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m]}\Theta_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{matrix}\Sigma^{m}_{k}\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}0_{[k],[k+m-1]}\\
\vdots\\
0_{[k+m-2],[k+m+1]}\\
\mathbb{I}_{[k+m+1]}\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\Sigma_{[k,m]}&\beta_{[k+m]}\end{array}\right).

Observe that (
3.7
) can be written

(T\beta)_{[k]}=(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m-1]}(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))^{+}_{[k+m-1],[k-1]}\\
+L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+m]}\Theta_{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{matrix}\Sigma^{m}_{k}\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}0_{[k],[k+m-1]}\\
\vdots\\
0_{[k+m-2],[k+m+1]}\\
\mathbb{I}_{[k+m+1]}\end{matrix}\\
\hline\cr\Sigma_{[k,m]}&\beta_{[k+m]}\end{array}\right)(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))^{+}_{[k+m-1],[k-1]}.

whenever the right inverse
(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))^{+}_{[k+m-1],[k-1]}
of
(L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k-1],[k+m-1]}
exists.

Proposition 3.8
.

The evaluation of the Laurent polynomial
L
on the Jacobi matrices
\boldsymbol{J}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{D})^{\top}
or on its perturbations
T\boldsymbol{J}=(TJ_{1},\dots,TJ_{D})^{\top}

are linked to the typical Darboux
LU
and
UL
factorizations of the Jacobi and Darboux transformed Jacobi matrices; i.e.,

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{J})

\displaystyle=M\omega,

\displaystyle L(T\boldsymbol{J})

\displaystyle=\omega M,

respectively.

Proof.

From Definition
3.2
we get
\omega=(TS)S^{-1}SL(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})S^{-1}=(TS)L(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})(TS)^{-1}(TS)S^{-1}
and therefore, using Proposition
3.3
and Definition
2.22
we get
M\omega=L(\boldsymbol{J})
, from the first equality, and
\omega M=L(T\boldsymbol{J})
from the second equality.
∎

From the first equation in the previous Proposition we get

Proposition 3.9
.

The block truncations
(\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k]}
admit a
LU
factorization

\displaystyle(L(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k]}=M^{[k]}\omega^{[k]}

in terms of the corresponding truncations of the adjoint resolvent
M^{[k]}
and resolvent
\omega^{[k]}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The truncated matrix
(L(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k]}
is regular and

\displaystyle\det\big((L(\boldsymbol{J}))^{[k]}\big)=\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}\frac{\det TH_{[l]}}{\det H_{[l]}}.

Proof.

To prove this result just use Propositions
3.8
, the explicit expression

\displaystyle\omega_{[k],[k]}=(TH_{[k]})H_{[k]}^{-1},

and the assumption that
the minors of the moment matrix and the perturbed moment matrix are not zero.
∎

3.3.
Nice Laurent polynomials. Flavors from tropical geometry

So far, for the analysis of Darboux transformations of MVOLPUT, we have followed the approach given in
[
14
]
for MVOPR. However, for the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{D}]
we had an ordering, the graded lexicographical order, wich is also a grading:
\deg(PQ)=\deg P+\deg Q
which is missing in this context. Now, for the Laurent polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,z_{D}^{\pm 1}]
, what we have is the longitude given in Definition
2.2
which as we already stressed does not give a grading to the ring. You can readily check that for two units in different orthants
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}|<|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}|
.
For example, for
D=2
the Laurent polynomial
L(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}^{-2}+z_{2}^{-2}+1
is of that type, observing
z_{1}z_{2}L(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}+z_{1}+z_{1}z_{2}
we deduce

\ell(z_{1}z_{2}L)=2<\ell(z_{1}z_{2})+\ell(L)=3
. However, there are other polynomials that do satisfy this important property, for example
L(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}^{-2}+z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}
. Indeed,
z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}L=z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-2}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+2}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}+1}
and
\ell(z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}L)=\max(|\alpha_{1}-2|+|\alpha_{2}|,|\alpha_{1}+2|+|\alpha_{2}|,|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}+1|)=|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+2
.

Definition 3.7
.

A Laurent polynomial
L
is nice if

\displaystyle\ell(LM)=\ell(L)+\ell(M),

for every Laurent polynomial
M\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,z_{D}^{\pm 1}]
.

Proposition 3.11
.

If
(V_{a,+}^{(m)},V_{a,-}^{(m)})\subset\operatorname{NP}(L)
with
\ell(L)=m
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{D}
, then
L
is nice.

Proof.

Remember that

\displaystyle\ell(z^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}L)=\max_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{NP}(L)\cap\mathbb{Z}^{D}}(|\alpha^{\prime}_{1}+\alpha_{1}|+\dots+|\alpha_{D}^{\prime}+\alpha_{D}|)\leq|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+\ell(L),

and if a pair of opposed vertices are included in the Newton polytope, say
V^{(m)}_{1,\pm}
, as
\max(|m+\alpha_{1}|,|-m+\alpha_{1}|)=|m|+|\alpha_{1}|
, the bound is saturated

\displaystyle\ell(z^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}L)=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|+\ell(L).

∎

However, these Laurent polynomials are not the only nice Laurent polynomials, for example
L=z_{1}^{-2}+z_{1}z_{2}^{-1}+z_{1}z_{2}
is also nice. Indeed,
z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}L=z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-2}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+1}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}+z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+1}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}+1}
and
\ell(z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}L)=\max(|\alpha_{1}-2|+|\alpha_{2}|,|\alpha_{1}+1|+|\alpha_{2}-1|,|\alpha_{1}+1|+|\alpha_{2}+1|)=|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+2
.
Another example is
L=\operatorname{i}z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}+2z_{1}z_{2}-\operatorname{i}z_{1}z_{2}^{-1}+2z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}^{-2}+z_{1}+z_{1}^{-1}+5
, in this case we
have

\ell(z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}z_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}L)=\max(|\alpha_{1}-1|+|\alpha_{2}+1|,|\alpha_{1}+1|+|\alpha_{2}+1|,|\alpha_{1}+1|+|\alpha_{2}-1|,|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}-1|)=|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+2.

The following is taken from tropical geometry
[
65
]

Definition 3.8
.

(1)

The Minkowski sum of the two sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
is given by

\displaystyle A+B=\{a+b:a\in A,b\in B\}.

(2)

For each
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
we can introduce an alternative order in
\mathbb{Z}^{D}
and say
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}>_{\boldsymbol{w}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}
if and only if
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime})\cdot\boldsymbol{w}>0
.

(3)

The initial form along the weight
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
of a Laurent polynomial is

\displaystyle\operatorname{in}_{\boldsymbol{w}}L\coloneq\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{w}}}L_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},

here the support is
\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{w}}\coloneq\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{A}:{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}\geq_{\boldsymbol{w}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\;\forall{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\}
.

(4)

The tropicalization of a Laurent polynomial also known as its tropical hypersurface is

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(L)\coloneq\{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}:\operatorname{in}_{\boldsymbol{w}}(L)\text{ is not a monomial}\}.

Proposition 3.12
.

(1)

For two Laurent polynomials
K,L\in\mathbb{C}[\boldsymbol{z}^{\pm 1}]
we have the following formulæ for their Newton polytopes and faces in terms of the Minkowski sum (the tropical product of polytopes)

(3.12)

\displaystyle\operatorname{NP}(KL)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{NP}(K)+\operatorname{NP}(L),

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(KL)=

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(K)+F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(L).

(2)

The Newton polytope
\operatorname{NP}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}L)
and its faces are obtained from the Newton polytope
\operatorname{NP}(L)
via an automorphism in the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{D}
–a translation by
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
–

\displaystyle\operatorname{NP}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}L)

\displaystyle=\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}+\operatorname{NP}(L),

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(z^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}L)

\displaystyle=\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}+F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(L).

(3)

Newton polytopes of initial forms are the faces of the Newton polytopes

\displaystyle\operatorname{NP}(\operatorname{in}_{\boldsymbol{w}}(L))=F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(\operatorname{NP}(L)).

(4)

The tropical hypersurface of a Laurent polynomial is

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(L)=\big\{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}:\dim F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(L)\geq 1\big\}.

The orthants of integers introduced in Definition
2.16
are extended to the real space as follows

Definition 3.9
.

The real orthants in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
are

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}\coloneq

\displaystyle\bigtimes_{i=1}^{D}R_{i},

\displaystyle R_{i}\coloneq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}_{-},&i\in(\sigma\setminus\partial\sigma),\\
\mathbb{R}_{<},&i\in\partial\sigma,\\
\mathbb{R}_{+},&i\in(\complement\sigma\setminus\partial(\complement\sigma)),\\
\mathbb{R}_{>},&i\in\partial\complement\sigma,\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{R}_{\gtrless}\coloneq\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\setminus\{0\}
. For
\sigma=\mathbb{Z}
we define

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}\coloneq\big(\mathbb{R}_{-}\big)^{D}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}.

In this case we do not need to distinguish between
\mathbb{R}_{\gtrless}
and its topological closure
\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\gtrless}}=\mathbb{R}_{\pm}
, but for consistency with previous developments we use a similar notation.

Proposition 3.13
.

Among the set of faces
\{F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(m):\dim F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(m)\geq 0\}_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}
of the regular hyper-octahedron
\operatorname{Con}([m])
there are
2^{D}
facets
\{F_{\sigma}(m)\}_{\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}}
, with a facet per orthant
F_{\sigma}(m)\subset(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}
. Non facet faces of codimension
p\in\{2,\dots,D\}
belong to the intersection
p
orthants.

Definition 3.10
.

We introduce
\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma}(L)\coloneq(\operatorname{NP}(L))\cap\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

A Laurent polynomial
L
is nice if and only if
\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma}(L)
contains elements of longitude
\ell(L)
for each
\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.6
.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

If a Laurent polynomial
L
is nice then its tropical hypersurface
\mathcal{T}(L)
has a non trivial intersection with each orthant

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(L)\cap\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}\neq

\displaystyle\emptyset,

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}.

Proposition 3.16
.

If a Laurent polynomial
L
is nice the polytopes corresponding to the translations by the generators of
\mathbb{Z}^{D}
of its Newton polytope,
\{\pm\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\}+\operatorname{NP}(L)
have elements of longitude
\ell(L)+1
for each
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{D}
.

Proof.

Let us suppose that a Laurent polynomial
L
is nice. Then, for each subset
\sigma\subset\{1,\dots,D\}
we have a vector
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}\in(\operatorname{NP}(L))\cap\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
with
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}|=\ell(L)
. Therefore,
as
\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\in\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
for
a\in\complement\sigma
and
-\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\in\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}

for
a\in\sigma
we have
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{e}_{a}|=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{a}|+1
for all
a\in\complement\sigma
and

|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{e}_{a}|=|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{a}|+1
whenever
a\in\sigma
. Therefore, as this happens for each orthant, we conclude that
\big(\{\pm\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\}+\operatorname{NP}(L)\big)\cap\{F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(\operatorname{Conv}[\ell(L)+1])\}_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}\neq\emptyset
.
∎

The previous two results are not characterizations of nicety but properties of Laurent polynomials that indeed could have non-nice Laurent polynomials. We now give a similar result to the last one that in fact is a characterization. For that aim we need to introduce
some geometrical elements of the regular hyper-octahedron

Definition 3.11
.

In each orthant
\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
we consider the vectors

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}=\sum_{a\in\complement\sigma}\boldsymbol{e}_{a}-\sum_{a\in\sigma}\boldsymbol{e}_{a}.

Proposition 3.17
.

The facets of the regular hyper-octahedron
\operatorname{Conv}([m])
are given by
F_{\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}}(\operatorname{Conv}([m]))
.

Proposition 3.18
.

A Laurent polynomial
L
is nice if and only if
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}\}+\operatorname{NP}(L)
has elements of longitude
\ell(L)+D
for each
\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
For a nice Laurent polynomial in each orthant we have a vector such that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}\in(\operatorname{NP}(L))\cap\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
with
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}|=\ell(L)
. Therefore,
as
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}\in\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
so that
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}|=\ell(L)+D
. As this happens for each orthant, we deduce that
\big(\{\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}\}+\operatorname{NP}(L)\big)\cap\{F_{\boldsymbol{w}}(\operatorname{Conv}[\ell(L)+D])\}_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}}\neq\emptyset
.

\Leftarrow

We proceed by contradiction and suppose that there is no such point in a given orthant
\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}
. Consequently, the set
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}\}+\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma}(L)
contains only vectors of longitude less than
\ell(L)+D
. But this also holds for the rest of orthants component of the Newton polytope
\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(L)
,
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
, as in that case, despite having vectors in the Newton polytope of the maximum longitude
\ell(L)
, when we consider the set
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}\}+\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(L)
being the vector
\boldsymbol{u}_{\sigma}
and the vectors in
\operatorname{NP}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(L)
in different orthants, some of the components are summations but some are subtractions, and the bound
\ell(L)+D
is never reached.
∎

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
L=L_{1}L_{2}
be the product of two Laurent polynomials. Then, the Laurent polynomial
L
is nice if and only if
L_{1},L_{2}
are nice.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.7
.
∎

Corollary 3.1
.

The set of nice polynomials is a monoid under the polynomial multiplication.

In the next picture we illustrate this situation, we have drawn
\operatorname{Conv}([k])
, for
k=0,\dots,4
, in dashed lines, and also the nice Newton polytopes
\operatorname{NP}(z_{1}^{-2}+z_{1}z_{2}^{-1}+z_{1}z_{2})
,

\operatorname{NP}(\operatorname{i}z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}+2z_{1}z_{2}-\operatorname{i}z_{1}z_{2}^{-1}+2z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}^{-2}+z_{1}+z_{1}^{-1}+9)
and
\operatorname{NP}(z_{1}^{-2}+z_{1}^{2}+z_{2})
in different colors, red, brown and yellow, respectively.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\operatorname{Conv}([1])
\operatorname{Conv}([2])
\operatorname{Conv}([3])
\operatorname{Conv}([4])

We now show the nice Newton polytopes and tropical curves in blue for basic degree 2 nice Laurent polynomials, any other one can be obtained from these by the corresponding convex hull of the unions of these basic Newton polytopes –observe that the two nice Newton polytopes of vertex type are not drawn here–

We show now some non basic nice Newton polytopes, and their tropical curves, built up by the convex hull of the union of the Newton polytopes of basic nice Laurent polynomials
\alpha_{1}

In the next picture we illustrate a case for
D=3
, we draw two regular octahedra, the interior one corresponds to the convex hull of
[1]
and the exterior one is the octahedron corresponding to
[2]
; blue points are the multi-indices. Then, in red, we draw the Newton polytope of the nice Laurent polynomial, which is not a vertex pair polynomial,
L=z_{1}z_{3}+2z_{1}^{-1}z_{3}-z_{2}z_{3}-z_{2}^{-1}z_{3}+3z_{3}^{-2}
