.

4.2.
Miwa type expressions for the MVOLPUT

We introduce the matrices

\displaystyle\alpha_{a,[k]}

\displaystyle\coloneq(T_{a}H_{[k]})H^{-1}_{[k]},

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}^{\dagger}_{a,[k]}

\displaystyle\coloneq H^{-1}_{[k]}T_{a}H_{[k]}.

In terms of which
the nonzero degree one resolvent block entries are

\displaystyle(\omega_{a})_{[k],[k]}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{a,[k]},

\displaystyle(\hat{\omega}_{a})_{[k],[k]}

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}_{a,[k]}

on the main diagonal, and
({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{+}
for
\omega_{a}
and
({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{+}
for
\hat{\omega}_{a}
, on the first superdiagonal.
The adjoint resolvent matrices are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{a}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\coloneq T_{a}H^{-1}\omega_{a}H=\big(\hat{S}(T_{a}\hat{S})^{-1}\big)^{\dagger},

\displaystyle M_{a}

\displaystyle:=H\hat{\omega}_{a}^{\dagger}T_{a}H^{-1}=S(T_{a}S)^{-1},

and consequently can be written as follows

\displaystyle M_{a}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}+H({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{-}T_{a}H^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{a}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}+(T_{a}H)^{-1}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{+}H.

Hence, these block unitriangular matrices have only two block diagonals different from zero, the main diagonal which equals the identity and the first sub-diagonal with

\displaystyle(M_{a})_{[k+1],[k]}

\displaystyle=\rho_{a,[k+1]},

\displaystyle(\hat{M}_{a})_{[k+1],[k]}

\displaystyle=\hat{\rho}_{a,[k+1]},

where

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\dagger}_{a,[k+1]}

\displaystyle\coloneq(T_{a}H_{[k]}^{-1})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}H_{[k+1]},

\displaystyle\rho_{a,[k+1]}

\displaystyle:=H_{[k+1]}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{[k+1],[k]}T_{a}H_{[k]}^{-1}.

As we know, we have for the transformed and non-transformed MVOLPUT the following connection formulæ

\displaystyle\omega_{a}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=(L_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}(\boldsymbol{z})-q_{a})T_{a}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{a}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=(L_{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}(\boldsymbol{z})-\bar{q}_{a})T_{a}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{a}T_{a}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}),

\displaystyle M_{a}T_{a}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}).

Now we need two technical lemmata

Lemma 4.1
.

The inequality
2D|[k]|\geqslant|[k+1]|
holds true for all
k=0,1,\dots
.

Proof.

Given a multi-index
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{D})\in[k]
such that its
i
-th component satisfies
\alpha_{i}\gtrless 0
, then
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\pm\boldsymbol{e_{i}}\in[k+1]
.
Therefore, for each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[k]
and each of its
D
components we can get one (
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
) or two (
\alpha_{i}=0
)
multi-indices in
[k+1]
out of the initial
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[k]
.
Hence, it is clear that given any multi-index
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[k]
we can get
n
multi-indices in
[k+1]
with
D\leqslant n\leqslant 2D
.
Now, we observe that any
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}\in[k+1]
can be obtained by this procedure form at least one multi-index in
[k]
. Then
n|[k]|\geqslant|[k+1]|
and consequently
2D|[k]|\geqslant|[k+1]|
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

The matrix

\displaystyle\big(\chi_{[1]}(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon})\big)_{[k],[k+1]}\coloneq\left(\begin{matrix}(\Upsilon_{1}^{-1})_{[k],[k+1]}\\
(\Upsilon_{2}^{-1})_{[k],[k+1]}\\
\vdots\\
(\Upsilon_{D}^{-1})_{[k],[k+1]}\\
(\Upsilon_{D})_{[k],[k+1]}\\
\vdots\\
(\Upsilon_{1})_{[k],[k+1]}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2D|[k]|\times|[k+1]|}

has full column rank for all
k=0,1\dots
.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.8

∎

Definition 4.2
.

For the degree one discrete flows we consider

(1)

The matrix

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\coloneq\left(\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{n}_{1}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{2D}^{\top}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2D\times 2D},

which we assume to be invertible (as a consecuence
\hat{N}=\bar{N}\mathcal{E}_{2D}
is also be invertible).

(2)

For any vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{D}
the associated vector

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\coloneq N\chi_{[1]}(\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{2D}

(3)

We consider two rectangular matrices

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left(\begin{matrix}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{1})_{[k],[k+1]}\\
\vdots\\
({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{2D})_{[k],[k+1]}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2D|[k]|\times|[k+1]|},

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k}

\displaystyle:=\left(\begin{matrix}T_{1}H_{[k]}\\
\vdots\\
T_{2D}H_{[k]}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2D|[k]|\times|[k]|}.

We are ready for the following result expressing the MVOLPUT as products of the quasi-tau matrices
H
and its discrete flows

Theorem 4.1
.

The MVOLPUT can be expressed in terms of quasi-tau matrices
H
and its degree one discrete flows as follows

\displaystyle\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k-1}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-1}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k-2}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-2}H_{[k-2]}^{-1}\cdots{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{0}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{0}H_{[0]}^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{[k]}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1})=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}}_{k-1}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-1}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}}_{k-2}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-2}H_{[k-2]}^{-1}\cdots{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{N}}}}}_{0}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{0}H_{[0]}^{-1}.

Proof.

Since
N
is invertible the matrix
{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}
still has full column rank. Hence,
the correlation matrix
{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{|[k+1]|\times|[k+1]|}
is invertible
and the left inverse of
{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}
is

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}^{+}\coloneq\big({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}^{\top}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}\big)^{-1}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k}^{\top}.

All the previous said, and as long as the two previously stated conditions hold we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{[k]}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})+(\boldsymbol{n}_{a}\cdot\chi_{[1]}(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}))_{[k],[k+1]}\phi_{[k+1]}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=0,

whenever the transformation parameters
\{\boldsymbol{n}_{a},q_{a}\}_{a=1}^{2D}
are as described in Definition
4.2
and therefore

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k-1}\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=-[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-1}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z})

But, since
{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k-1}
has full column rank
|[k]|
with
left inverse
{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{k-1}^{+}
we get

\displaystyle\phi_{[k]}(\boldsymbol{z})=-{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.56248pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.54236pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.96182pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{N}}}}_{(k-1)}^{+}[\boldsymbol{T}H]_{k-1}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\phi_{[k-1]}(\boldsymbol{z}).

It is easy to see that a similar result can be obtained for
\hat{\phi}
when evaluated
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1}
.
Iteration leads to the desired result.
∎

4.3.
A Toda type integrable hierarchy

For the continuous deformation we introduce a covector
t:=(t_{[0]},t_{[1]},\dots)
where

t_{[k]}=\big(t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{(k)}_{1}},\dots,t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{(k)}_{|[k]|}}\big)
are complex covectors as well.

Definition 4.3
.

The vacuum wave matrix is

\displaystyle W_{0}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneq\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})},

\displaystyle t(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}t_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}.

Then

Proposition 4.4
.

The matrix
G(t)=W_{0}(t)G=GW_{0}(t)
is the moment matrix of the deformed measure

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}\mu_{t}=\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})}\operatorname{d}\mu,

and the Fourier series of the evolved measure is

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{t}=\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})}\hat{\mu}.

Definition 4.4
.

We say that
t
is an admissible set of times if
G(t)
admits a block Cholesky factorization as in Proposition
2.4
.

Hereon we assume that we are dealing with admissible times.

For the continuous flows we need
\exp(t(\boldsymbol{z}))
to be real which is achieved when
t(\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathbb{R}
, this last requirement holds for
t

such that
\bar{t}=t\eta
; then, we request
\bar{t}_{[k]}=t_{[k]}\mathcal{E}_{|[k]|}
or, equivalently,

Proposition 4.5
.

The continuous flows preserve the reality and definite positiveness whenever

t_{-\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\bar{t}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. The real deformed measure in
\mathbb{T}^{D}
has the following form

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}\mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\operatorname{e}^{2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{|[k]|/2}t_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(k)}_{j}}\cos(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(k)}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta})}\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

From the Gauss–Borel factorization of the perturbed measure

(4.4)

\displaystyle W_{0}(t)G=GW_{0}(t)=G(t)

\displaystyle=(S(t))^{-1}H(t)\big((\hat{S}(t))^{-1}\big)^{\dagger}

we get
t
-dependent MVOLPUT.

Definition 4.5
.

The wave matrices are defined as follows

\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle:=S(t)W_{0}(t),

\displaystyle\hat{W}_{1}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=W_{0}(t)\big(\hat{S}(t)\big)^{\dagger},

\displaystyle W_{2}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\big(S(t)\big)^{-1}H(t),

\displaystyle\hat{W}_{2}

\displaystyle:=H(t)\Big(\big(\hat{S}(t)\big)^{-1}\Big)^{\dagger}.

Proposition 4.6
.

We have

(4.5)

\displaystyle G=W_{1}^{-1}\hat{W_{2}}=W_{2}^{\dagger}\left(\hat{W}^{-1}_{1}\right)^{\dagger}.

Proof.

Use the Gauss–Borel factorization of

G({t})=W_{0}({t})G=GW_{0}({t})
.
∎

The splitting as a direct sum of the linear semi-infinite matrices in strictly block lower triangular matrices and upper block triangular
matrices is denoted by
M=M_{<}+M_{\geq}
, and the associated splitting in strictly upper triangular and lower triangular parts is denoted
by
M=M_{\leq}+M_{>}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

We have the Gel’fand–Dickey expressions

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}S^{-1}+S\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big(S\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{-1}\Big)_{\geq},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}S^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\Big(S\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{-1}\Big)_{<},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{S}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\hat{S}^{-1}+\hat{S}\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\hat{S}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big(\hat{S}\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{S}^{-1}\Big)_{\geq},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{S}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\hat{S}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\Big(\hat{S}\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{S}^{-1}\Big)_{<}.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.9
.
∎

Observe that for Hermitian cases the first and second lines in the previous lemma coincide —due to the equality between hatted and non hatted terms and

t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
.

Definition 4.6
.

We introduce the Zakharov–Shabat matrices

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=

\displaystyle(J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(t))_{\geq},

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=(\hat{J}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(t))_{\geq}.

The next important result collects the essential elements of the associated integrable theory

Proposition 4.7
.

(1)

The wave matrices solve the linear systems

(4.6)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial W_{1}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}W_{1},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{W}_{1}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{W}_{1}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{W}_{2}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle B_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{W}_{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial W_{2}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}W_{2}

(2)

The Jacobi matrices are
Lax
matrices; i.e, they fulfill the following Lax equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}=

\displaystyle[B_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}},J_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}],

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{J}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}=

\displaystyle[\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}},\hat{J}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}].

(3)

The Zakharov–Shabat (or zero-curvature) equations are satisfied

\displaystyle\frac{\partial B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}-\frac{\partial B_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}+\big[B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},B_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}\big]=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}-\frac{\partial\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}+\big[\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}},\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime}}\big]=

\displaystyle 0.

Proof.

See Appendix
C.10
.
∎

Notice that only the equations on the left or the right would be need in the Hermitian case.

We now introduce the Baker functions and their adjoints

Definition 4.7
.

The Baker and adjoint Baker wave functions are

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=W_{1}\chi,

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\left(W_{1}^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}\chi,

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\chi^{\dagger}\hat{W}_{1}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\left(\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\chi^{\dagger}\hat{W}_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\hat{W}_{2}\chi,

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\left(\hat{W}_{2}^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}\chi,

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\chi^{\dagger}W_{2}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\left(\Psi_{2}^{*}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\chi^{\dagger}W_{2}^{-1}.

Proposition 4.8
.

The Baker functions and adjoint Baker functions in Definition
4.7
can be expressed in terms of the MVOLPUT and the Fourier transform of the measure as follows

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D},

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{D}\bar{\hat{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})\Big(\big(H(t)\big)^{-1}\Big)^{\dagger}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{-1}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{D}_{\mu},

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{e}^{\bar{t}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D},

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{D}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})\big(H(t)\big)^{-1}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{D}_{\mu},

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{D}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{D}_{\mu},

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle=\Big(\big(H(t)\big)^{-1}\Big)^{\dagger}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{D}\bar{\hat{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})\operatorname{e}^{\bar{t}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{-1}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{D}_{\mu},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle=\big(H(t)\big)^{-1}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{D}.

Proof.

We use Definitions
2.10
,
4.5
and
4.7
together with Proposition
2.10

∎

Proposition 4.9
.

The Baker functions satisfy the following linear differential equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\Psi_{1},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{1}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat{\Psi}_{1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{2}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\hat{\Psi}_{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{2}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Psi_{2}

while the adjoint Baker functions satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle-\left(\hat{B}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right)^{\dagger}\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}^{*}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle-\left(B_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right)^{\dagger}\Psi_{1}^{*},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{2}^{*}}{\partial t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle-\left(\hat{B}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right)^{\dagger}\Psi_{2}^{*},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}=

\displaystyle-\left(B_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right)^{\dagger}\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}.

Proof.

Taking derivatives on their definitions and using Lemma
4.3
the result is straightforward. ∎

We will be specially interested in the
t_{[1]}
part of the covector of the continuous deformation parameters

t_{[1]}=(t_{-1},\dots,t_{-D},t_{D},\dots,t_{1}).

Proposition 4.10
.

The following differential equations are satisfied by the square matrices
H_{[k]}
and the rectangular matrices
\beta_{[k]}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\beta_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}}

\displaystyle=-H_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\beta}_{[k]}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-a}}

\displaystyle=-H_{[k]}^{\dagger}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}\left(H_{[k-1]}^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}}H_{[k]}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\beta_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k-1],[k]}-(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k-1]}\beta_{[k+1]}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H_{[0]}}{\partial t_{a}}H_{[0]}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-(\Upsilon_{a})_{[0]}{[1]}\beta_{[1]}

Proof.

These relations are just identifications if we use in Lemma
4.3
the explicit formulæ (
2.14
).
∎

Taking second partial derivatives in the previous expressions leads to the following

Theorem 4.2
.

The
H_{[k]}
matrices are subject to the following Toda lattice type equations

(4.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{b}}\left(\frac{\partial H_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}}H_{[k]}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k],[k+1]}H_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k+1],[k]}H_{[k]}^{-1}-H_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k],[k-1]}H_{[k-1]}^{-1}(\Upsilon_{a})_{[k-1],[k]}

which can be rewritten for the rectangular matrices
\beta_{[k]}
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\beta_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}\partial t_{b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{a}}\left(\beta_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k-1],[k]}\beta_{[k]}\right)-\frac{\partial\beta_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}}\beta_{[k-1]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k-2],[k-1]}-(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k],[k+1]}\beta_{[k+1]}\frac{\partial\beta_{[k]}}{\partial t_{a}}

and for the
\hat{\beta}_{[k]}
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\hat{\beta}_{[k]}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-a}\partial\bar{t}_{-b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}_{-a}}\left(\hat{\beta}_{[k]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k-1],[k]}\hat{\beta}_{[k]}\right)-\frac{\partial\hat{\beta}_{[k]}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-a}}\hat{\beta}_{[k-1]}(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k-2],[k-1]}-(\Upsilon_{b})_{[k],[k+1]}\hat{\beta}_{[k+1]}\frac{\partial\hat{\beta}_{[k]}}{\partial\bar{t}_{-a}}.

4.4.
Bilinear equations

We begin with the following observation

Proposition 4.11
.

Wave matrices evaluated at different admissible times
t,t^{\prime}
are related as it follows

\displaystyle W_{1}(t)\left(W_{1}(t^{\prime})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\hat{W}_{2}(t)\left(\hat{W}_{2}(t^{\prime})\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\hat{W}_{1}(t)\left(\hat{W}_{1}(t^{\prime})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=W_{2}(t)\left(W_{2}(t^{\prime})\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle W_{1}(t)\left(W_{2}(t^{\prime})\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\hat{W}_{2}(t)\left(\hat{W}_{1}(t^{\prime})\right)^{\dagger}.

Proof.

Just remember
4.5
wich is valid when evaluated at any time, therefore

\displaystyle G=\left(W_{1}(t)\right)^{-1}\hat{W}_{2}(t)=\left(W_{2}(t)\right)^{\dagger}\left([\hat{W}_{1}(t)]^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}=\left(W_{1}(t^{\prime})\right)^{-1}\hat{W}_{2}(t^{\prime})=\left(W_{2}(t^{\prime})\right)^{\dagger}\left([\hat{W}_{1}(t^{\prime})]^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}

from where each equality follows easily.
∎

Notice that the first two relations would be equivalent in the Hermitian case.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any polyradius
\boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}_{>}^{D}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\big(2\pi\operatorname{i}\big)^{D}}\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r})}\frac{\chi(\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\left(\chi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})\right)^{\top}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}=\frac{1}{\big(2\pi\operatorname{i}\big)^{D}}\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r})}\frac{\chi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})\cdot\left(\chi(\boldsymbol{z})\right)^{\top}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

here
\operatorname{d}\boldsymbol{z}\coloneq\operatorname{d}z_{1}\operatorname{d}z_{2}\dots\operatorname{d}z_{D}
denotes de Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{C}^{D}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let

\boldsymbol{c},\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mu}
,

i\in\{1,2\}

be two points in the Reinhardt domain of convergence of the Laurent series
\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})
of the measure
\operatorname{d}\mu
and let
\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}},\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>}^{D}
be the corresponding
poly-radii. Then, the Baker functions satisfy the following bilinear equations for every couple of admissible times
(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\frac{\Psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\Psi_{1}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\frac{\hat{\Psi}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\frac{\Psi_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\Psi_{2}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\ \oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\frac{\hat{\Psi}_{1}(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\frac{\Psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\Psi_{2}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\frac{\hat{\Psi}_{2}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Psi}_{1}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\frac{\Psi_{2}^{*}(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\Psi_{1}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\frac{\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}.

The MVOLPUT evaluated at different times satisfy

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z})}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{t(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\Phi(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{\bar{t}(\boldsymbol{z})}\bar{\hat{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{z})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\hat{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\Phi(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{\bar{t}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}\bar{\hat{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{(t+t^{\prime})(\boldsymbol{z})}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{(t+t^{\prime})(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z},

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\boldsymbol{c}})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{T}^{D}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{c}}}^{-1})}\Phi(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1},t)\big(\hat{\Phi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}},t^{\prime})\big)^{\dagger}\frac{\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z}^{-1})}{\prod_{a=1}^{D}z_{a}}\operatorname{d}^{D}\boldsymbol{z}.

Proof.

For the first set of equations involving Baker functions just insert the identity according to Lemma
4.4
matrix in each equality in
4.11
and remember Definition

4.7
. For the second set of equations involving MVOLPUT use Proposition
4.8
.
∎

4.5.
Miwa shifts and vertex operators

For
D=1
, see
[
7
]
, Miwa shifts generate discrete flows from continuous flows, there you have coherent shifts of the form
t\to t+[w]
or
t_{j}\to t_{j}+w^{j}/j
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>}
, where we request
w\in\mathbb{D},z\in\bar{\mathbb{D}}
or
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\bar{\mathbb{D}},z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus{\mathbb{D}}
; recall that when considered as a perturbation of the measure we evaluate
z\in\mathbb{T}
. This suggests to consider for each orthant different Miwa shifts.
We need to use a slightly modified version of Definitions
2.16
and
2.17
and also introduce orthant coherent Miwa shifts

Definition 4.8
.

For each subset
\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}
we introduce

(1)

The corresponding complete integer orthants are given by

\displaystyle(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}\coloneq

\displaystyle\bigtimes_{i=1}^{D}Z^{i},

\displaystyle Z^{i}\coloneq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{-},&i\in\sigma,\\
\mathbb{Z}_{+},&i\in\complement\sigma,\end{cases}

(2)

For any
\boldsymbol{w}\in(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}
the corresponding orthant Miwa coherent shift
[\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma}
has as its entries

\displaystyle([\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma})_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\coloneq\begin{cases}0,&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}\setminus(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma},\\
\frac{\boldsymbol{w}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|},&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}.\end{cases}

Observe that
(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}=\mathbb{Z}^{D}\cap\overline{(\mathbb{R}^{D})_{\sigma}}

Lemma 4.5
.

For each
\sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}_{D}
and
\boldsymbol{w}\in(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}
we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}([\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma})_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\log\big(1-\sum_{i\in\sigma}(w_{i}z_{i})^{-1}-\sum_{i\in\complement\sigma}w_{i}z_{i}\big)

uniformly for
\boldsymbol{z}\in\overline{(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

In the one hand we have

(4.9)

\displaystyle-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}([\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma})_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}}\frac{\boldsymbol{w}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{z}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}

and on the other the expansion

\displaystyle\log\big(1-\sum_{i\in\sigma}(w_{i}z_{i})^{-1}-\sum_{i\in\complement\sigma}w_{i}z_{i}\big)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\Big(\sum_{i\in\sigma}(w_{i}z_{i})^{-1}+\sum_{i\in\complement\sigma}w_{i}z_{i}\Big)^{k}

converges uniformly for
\boldsymbol{z}\in\overline{(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}}
and coincides with the RHS of (
4.9
).
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

For each
\sigma\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{D}}
and each
\boldsymbol{w}\in(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}
the orthant Miwa coherent shifts in the continuos flows

\displaystyle t\mapsto t-[\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma}

induce, in terms of the Laurent polynomial
L_{\boldsymbol{w}}\coloneq 1-\sum\limits_{i\in\sigma}w_{i}^{-1}z_{i}^{-1}-\sum\limits_{i\in\complement\sigma}w_{i}z_{i}
of longitude
\ell(L)=1

the following Darboux perturbation of the measure

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}\mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rightarrow L_{\boldsymbol{w}}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\boldsymbol{\theta}})\operatorname{d}\mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Proof.

From the previous Lemma we have

\displaystyle\exp(t(\boldsymbol{z})-[\boldsymbol{w}]_{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{z}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{z})=\big(1-\sum_{i\in\sigma}(w_{i}z_{i})^{-1}-\sum_{i\in\complement\sigma}w_{i}z_{i}\big)\exp(t(\boldsymbol{z}))\operatorname{d}\mu(\boldsymbol{z})

and the result follows.
∎

This Miwa shift can be written as a vertex type operator
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\sigma}
for each orthant
(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}
and each vector
\boldsymbol{w}\in(\mathbb{D}^{D})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\sigma}\coloneq\exp\Big(-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{D})^{\sigma}}\frac{\boldsymbol{w}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Big).

Observe that these vertex operators generate discrete flows or Darboux transformations. Consequently, they are useful in results like the Miwa expressions of the MVOLPUT, see Theorem
4.1

4.6.
Asymptotic modules

We will proceed to study families of nonlinear partial differential-difference equations
involving a single fixed site, say the
k
-th position, in the lattice and therefore not mixing several sites as the Toda type equations do mix, involving near neighbors
k-1
,
k
and
k+1
. .

Since we are interested in partial differential-difference equations in this section we have to consider the full deformation matrix,
this is, the product of both the continuous and discrete parts that have been introduced in previous sections.

Definition 4.9
.

For the perturbation parameters
t=\{t_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}
,
t_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2D}
we introduce the following
complete perturbation

\displaystyle W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\coloneq\exp\Big(\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}t_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Upsilon_{{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\Big)\prod_{a=1}^{2D}\left({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a}-q_{a}\right)^{m_{a}}.

Observe that

\displaystyle(W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m}))^{\dagger}=\exp(\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}}\bar{t}_{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\Upsilon_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}})\prod_{a=1}^{2D}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a}-\bar{q}_{a})^{m_{a}}

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial W_{0}^{\dagger}}{\partial\bar{t}_{a}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial W_{0}}{\partial t_{a}}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle T_{a}W_{0}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left(T_{a}W_{0}\right)^{\dagger}.

Definition 4.10
.

Given two semi-infinite matrices
R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
and
R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
we say that

•

R_{1}(t)\in\mathfrak{l}W_{0}
if
R_{1}(t)\big(W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}
is a block strictly lower triangular matrix.

•

R_{1}(t)\in(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}
if
R_{1}(t)\big((W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m}))^{\dagger}\big)^{-1}
is a block strictly lower triangular matrix.

•

R_{2}(t)\in\mathfrak{u}
if it is a block upper triangular matrix.

The sets
\mathfrak{l}W_{0}
and
\mathfrak{u}
are known as
asymptotic modules
.

Then, we can state the following
congruences

[
66
]
or
asymptotic module

[
54
]
style results

Proposition 4.13

(Asymptotic modules)
.

(1)

Given two semi-infinite matrices
R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
and
R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
such that

•

R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\in\mathfrak{l}W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
,

•

R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\in\mathfrak{u}
,

•

R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G=R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
.

Then,
R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=0
and
R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=0
.

(2)

Given two semi-infinite matrices
R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
and
R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
such that

•

R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\in(\mathfrak{l}W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m}))^{\dagger}
,

•

R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\in\mathfrak{u}
,

•

R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G^{\dagger}=R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})
.

Then,
R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=0
and
R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=0
.

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(W_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}W_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(W_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}\hat{W}_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m}),

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\Big(H(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big((\hat{S}(t,\boldsymbol{m}))^{-1}\big)^{\dagger}\Big)^{-1}=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}\big(S(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1},

and also that

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G^{\dagger}=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(\hat{W}_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}\hat{W}_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})G^{\dagger}=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(\hat{W}_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1}W_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m}),

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\Big(H(t,\boldsymbol{m})^{-1}S(t,\boldsymbol{m})\Big)^{\dagger}=R_{1}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big(W_{0}(t,\boldsymbol{m})^{\dagger}\big)^{-1}\big(\hat{S}(t,\boldsymbol{m})\big)^{-1},

and, as in every RHS we have a strictly lower triangular matrix while in every LHS we have an upper triangular matrix,
both sides must vanish and the result follows.
∎

We use the congruence notation

Definition 4.11
.

When
A-B\in\mathfrak{l}W_{0}

\left(\in(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}\right)

we write
A=B+\mathfrak{l}W_{0}

\left(A=B+(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}\right)
and if
A-B\in\mathfrak{u}
we write
A=B+\mathfrak{u}
.

4.7.
KP flows

We now study, using the asymptotic module technique, the first and second order KP flows and their compatibility conditions resulting in nonlinear partial differential-difference equations for the coefficients of the MVOLPUT. These are onsite equations not mixing different values of
k
. Finally, we consider the third order flows.

Definition 4.12
.

•

The longitude one perturbation parameters are

\displaystyle t_{[1]}\coloneq

\displaystyle(t_{-1},\dots,t_{-D},t_{D},\dots,t_{1}),

where

\displaystyle t_{a}

\displaystyle\coloneq t_{\operatorname{sgn}(a)\boldsymbol{e}_{|a|}},

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\in\{1,\dots,D\}.

•

We also consider the following derivatives

\displaystyle\partial_{a}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{a}}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{a}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}_{a}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{b=-D}^{D}{n}_{a,b}\partial_{b},

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{b=-D}^{D}{n}_{a,b}\bar{\partial}_{b},

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{a}\in\mathbb{C}^{2D}
,
a\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm D\}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

The following relations hold true

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}W_{1}=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+\beta)({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}\big]W_{0}+\mathfrak{l}W_{0},

\displaystyle T_{a}W_{1}=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+T_{a}\beta)({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}-q_{a}\big]W_{0}+\mathfrak{l}W_{0},

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}\hat{W}_{1}=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+\hat{\beta})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}\big]W_{0}^{\dagger}+(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger},

\displaystyle T_{a}\hat{W}_{1}=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+T_{a}\hat{\beta})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}-\bar{q}_{a}\big]W_{0}^{\dagger}+(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}.

Coinciding both equations in the Hermitian case.

Proof.

Firstly, we realize that

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}W_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a}W_{0},

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}W_{0}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a}W_{0}^{\dagger}.

Secondly, we remember the definitions of
W_{1}
and
\hat{W}_{1}
and work there using the congruence techniques

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}W_{1}=

\displaystyle[\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}S+S{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a}]W_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+\beta)({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}\big]W_{0}+\mathfrak{l}W_{0},

\displaystyle T_{a}W_{1}=

\displaystyle(T_{a}S)({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a}-q_{a})W_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+T_{a}\beta)({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}-q_{a}\big]W_{0}+\mathfrak{l}W_{0},

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}\hat{W}_{1}=

\displaystyle[\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}\hat{S}+\hat{S}{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a}]W_{0}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+\hat{\beta})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}\big]W_{0}^{\dagger}+(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger},

\displaystyle T_{a}\hat{W}_{1}=

\displaystyle(T_{a}\hat{S})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a}-\bar{q}_{a}\big)W_{0}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[(\mathbb{I}+T_{a}\hat{\beta})({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}-\bar{q}_{a}\big]W_{0}^{\dagger}+(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}.

∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Both Baker functions
\{\Psi_{1},\hat{\Psi}_{2}\}
are solutions of the following partial difference-differential linear systems

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}-T_{a}\big)\Psi=[q_{a}-\Delta_{a}\beta({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\Psi.

While both Baker functions
\{\hat{\Psi}_{1},\Psi_{2}\}
do solve

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}-T_{a}\big)\Psi=[\bar{q}_{a}-\Delta_{a}\hat{\beta}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\Psi.

Only one set of these equations is relevant in the Hermitian case.

Proof.

The results in Proposition
4.14
can be rewritten as it follows

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}-T_{a}-[q_{a}-\Delta_{a}\beta({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\big)W_{1}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{l}W_{0},

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}-T_{a}-[\bar{q}_{a}-\Delta_{a}\hat{\beta}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\big)\hat{W}_{1}

\displaystyle\in(\mathfrak{l}W_{0})^{\dagger}.

Then, it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}_{a}}-T_{a}-[q_{a}-\Delta_{a}\beta({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.5648pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.49536pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.7824pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\big)\hat{W}_{2}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{u},

\displaystyle\big(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{n}}_{a}}-T_{a}-[\bar{q}_{a}-\Delta_{a}\hat{\beta}({\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.77779pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}}}}}_{a})_{\geq}]\big)W_{2}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{u}.

Finally, just recall the definitions of the Baker functions and apply Proposition
4.13
.
∎

The compatibility of the linear systems satisfied by the Baker functions imply

Theorem 4.4
.

The following nonlinear discrete equations for the first subdiagonal matrices
\beta
and
